2. Add vanilla bean paste before the mix gets too hot.
3. Remove from heat and refrigerate for 3–4 hours until fully chilled.
4. Before churning, stir in the skimmed milk powder (2 tbsp).
5. Return to fridge for 20 minutes, then give a final stir.
6. Pre-cool your ice cream machine for 10 minutes (optional but recommended).
7. Churn for approximately 20 minutes until done.

Cookie Dough Core:
1. Heat-treat the flour in a microwave or oven (180°C for ~10 min) and allow to cool completely before use.
2. Beat softened butter and sugar together until well combined. Butter must be genuinely soft — hard butter will spray sugar everywhere.
3. Add salt, vanilla extract, and milk. Mix until combined.
4. Fold in the heat-treated flour and rainbow sprinkles.
5. Load the dough into a piping tube or roll into a cylinder shape wrapped in cling film.
6. Refrigerate until firm.

Coloured Chocolate Drops:
1. Pre-chill a flat baking pan in the freezer, lined or unlined.
2. Melt Deco Melts in the microwave until loose and fluid.
3. Dot or pipe the melted chocolate onto the frozen pan — a piping bag is much easier than a spoon.
4. Return the pan to the freezer for 5–10 minutes until fully set.
5. Scrape off the hardened drops and set aside in a container.

Assembly:
1. Fill the tub with churned ice cream, scattering in the coloured chocolate drops as you layer it up.
2. Press the chilled cookie dough cylinder straight down through the centre of the tub.
3. Top up any gaps with remaining ice cream.
4. Freeze for at least 7 hours (or overnight) until fully hardened.
5. Optional: line the tub with cling film before filling if you want to unmould for a cross-section reveal.

**Notes:**
- This is Nick's first attempt at the core and chocolate-drop technique — he calls it the "good, bad and ugly" version, so expect some trial and error.
- Use butter that is genuinely at room temperature for the cookie dough; cold butter will not incorporate well.
- Make more Deco Melt drops than you think you need — Nick made too few and regretted it.
- Deco Melts (baking candy melts) are the correct product to use; the B&J original labels them "chocolate flavour" not "chocolate", hence the substitute.
- The cookie dough core can also be formed without a tube: roll into a log, wrap in cling film, freeze solid, then press into the tub.
- Cookie dough recipe sourced from cookingclassy.com (no almond extract used).
---

### [Biscoff Cookies n Cream Ice Cream - Speculoos](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=LV7Du-aFQ1s) ice cream base loaded with chopped Biscoff sandwich cookies mixed in after churning.

**Ingredients:**

- **500ml** whole milk
- **400ml** cream
- **160g** sugar
- **200g** Biscoff spread
- **1 pack** Biscoff sandwich cookies (Lotus Biscoff — cookie rounds with biscuit cream filling)

**Steps:**

1. Combine milk and sugar in a saucepan. Heat gently, stirring, until the sugar is fully dissolved.
2. Add 200g Biscoff spread and whisk until fully incorporated into the milk-sugar base.
3. Remove from heat. Add the cream directly to the hot base — this drops the temperature quickly and reduces fridge time needed.
4. Transfer to a container and refrigerate for 1–2 hours until fully chilled.
5. Churn in an ice cream machine for approximately 10–30 minutes (time varies by machine) until done.
6. While churning, chop the Biscoff sandwich cookies into a mix of small pieces and larger chunks — avoid making them all uniformly fine.
7. Once churned, layer the ice cream into a tub, folding in generous amounts of chopped cookies throughout. Do not scrimp — use the whole pack.
8. Freeze for several hours until firm.

**Notes:**

- Nick notes this is a no-egg base — simpler than his usual custard method for this flavour.
- Adding cream to the hot base (rather than chilling the base first) is a quick-cool trick that cuts fridge time to 1–2 hours instead of longer.
- Biscoff sandwich cookies (cookie rounds with cream filling) are not available everywhere — substitute plain Lotus Biscoff biscuits if needed, though the sandwich version adds extra richness.
- Use the full pack of cookies and don't chop them too fine — having some larger chunks gives better texture contrast.
- The base is heavily Biscoff-flavoured, so pairing it with plenty of cookies makes the most of it.
---

### [Biscoff Gelato Recipe](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=3vvNSnfocok)

Traditional churned gelato using Biscoff spread — high sugar, low fat, small cream addition.

**Ingredients:**

- **600g** milk
- **50g** double/heavy cream
- **150g** sugar
- **50g** skimmed milk powder
- **100g** Biscoff spread
- **1.5–2g** stabilizer blend of choice

**Steps:**

1. Add milk to a saucepan. Mix stabilizers with a small portion of the sugar, then whisk into the cold milk.
2. Combine remaining sugar with the skimmed milk powder and add to the pan. (Mixing milk powder with sugar first helps it dissolve without clumping.)
3. Heat the mixture, stirring, until the Biscoff spread is fully dissolved in.
4. Once the mix reaches 60–65°C, add the cream and stir to combine.
5. Remove from heat and transfer to a container. Chill in the fridge for 3–4 hours until completely cold.
6. Give the base a final stir, then churn in an ice cream machine — Nick's took ~15–16 minutes.
7. Transfer to tubs and freeze for at least 4 hours before serving.
8. Remove from freezer 5–6 minutes before serving — gelato is served at a slightly warmer temperature than ice cream.

**Notes:**

- Gelato style: high sugar, low fat — distinct from the American-style Biscoff ice cream version Nick also made (linked in the description).
- Stabilizer is important for texture and longevity beyond 24 hours; without it the texture suffers on storage.
- If milk powder clumps when added to liquid, pre-mixing it with sugar dissolves it almost instantly.
- Biscoff is a caramelized, cinnamon-flavored cookie spread — the same biscuit found on hotel pillows and at breakfast buffets.

---

### [Black Forest Ice Cream using an Ice Cream Calculator](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PrpvcOiU7Qw)

Sweet cream ice cream base built with Scoopulator, finished with chopped dark chocolate and a canned Bing cherry swirl.

> **Batch recipe** — yields approximately 2+ pints.

---

**Ingredients — Ice Cream Base:**

- **634g** whipping cream (33% fat; US: heavy cream, UK: double cream)
- **634g** whole milk (3.2–3.25% fat)
- **135g** allulose (powdered or granulated — no difference)
- **25g** glucose syrup (plain corn syrup; not high-fructose corn syrup)
- **70g** skim milk powder
- **1.9g** stabilizer (any standard ice cream stabilizer blend)

**Ingredients — Cherry Swirl:**

- **1 can** Bing cherries in syrup (400ml can; ~42g sugar per 167ml)
- **35g** glucose syrup (plain corn syrup)
- **10g** sugar

**Ingredients — Mix-In:**

- Dark chocolate (amount to taste — chopped finely before adding)

---

**Steps:**

1. Combine milk, cream, glucose syrup, and skim milk powder in a saucepan.
2. In a separate small bowl, mix the stabilizer into the allulose powder, then stir the allulose-stabilizer blend into the base. Mixing stabilizer with dry sugar first prevents clumping.
3. Heat the base to 85°C (185°F), stirring throughout — takes about 5–10 minutes depending on heat level.
4. Cool the base in the refrigerator and age overnight. This builds milk flavour and allows the stabilizer to fully distribute through the mix.
5. For the cherry swirl: pour the entire can of Bing cherries (cherries + syrup) into a saucepan. Heat on a gentle flame to 85–90°C and hold for 5–10 minutes, reducing the weight by 30–40%.
6. Let the reduced cherries cool for about 30 minutes, then blend smooth (blender, stick blender, or food processor).
7. Add 35g glucose syrup and 10g sugar to the blended cherries, stir together, then return to heat and bring back to 85°C for 2 minutes. Cool and refrigerate overnight to thicken and develop flavour.
8. Before churning, place the chocolate in the freezer — cold chocolate chops cleanly with less static fly-off.
9. Remove the aged base from the fridge, stir well, then churn in your ice cream machine.
10. While the base is churning, finely chop the chocolate.
11. Load churned ice cream into tubs, layering in chopped chocolate and spooning the cherry swirl through the ice cream as you go.
12. Freeze until firm.

---

**Notes:**

- Allulose has ~70% the sweetness of sucrose and a high freezing point depression effect (FPD), which means you can use less of it than sugar — this leaves total solids lower than ideal. Nick targets ~40% solids; this recipe comes in slightly below at ~36–37% due to the allulose. The stabilizer compensates by increasing viscosity and creating the perception of higher solids.
- Glucose syrup (corn syrup) is not very sweet on its own (~40 DE) and contributes body and ice crystal control in both the base and the cherry swirl — it slows ice crystal growth so the swirl doesn't freeze rock-hard.
- The sugar in the cherry swirl adjusts sweetness and provides additional FPD to keep the swirl soft.
- Heating the base to 85°C is required specifically because of the stabilizer; without stabilizer, that temperature isn't strictly necessary but still improves results.
- The Scoopulator target used was "general ice cream" profile, aimed at 14–15% fat. Scoopability target was -4°C serving point (mid-range of the scoopability dial).
- Nick notes the natural canned-cherry swirl has a milder flavour compared to commercial cherry syrups (e.g., Amarena) — you can thicken it further with a small amount of guar or xanthan gum if a thicker swirl is preferred.
- The chocolate is added as a frozen chopped chunk mix-in, not stirred in during churning, so it does not create a stracciatella effect — the pieces stay distinct.

---

### [Burnt Basque Cheesecake Ice Cream](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=1DU_V3sqmiM)

A lemon cheesecake ice cream base loaded with large cubes of homemade burnt basque cheesecake. Batch recipe (traditional ice cream machine, not Ninja Creami).

**Ingredients:**

Ice Cream Base:
- **550g** milk, 1.8% fat
- **150g** cream, 50% fat
- **250g** mascarpone cheese, 40–45% fat
- **150g** sugar
- **75g** dextrose
- **65g** skimmed milk powder
- **5g** lemon extract (Nelson Massey brand used)
- **2g** stabiliser (optional)

Burnt Basque Cheesecake:
- **600g** cream cheese (e.g. Philadelphia), room temperature
- **4** eggs
- **225g** sugar
- **40g** plain flour
- **300g** double cream
- **5ml** vanilla extract (optional)
- Pinch of salt

**Steps:**

Burnt Basque Cheesecake:
1. Bring cream cheese to room temperature. Place in a bowl, add sugar, and mix until smooth and well combined — do not whip.
2. Add eggs one at a time, mixing just until combined after each — no whipping.
3. Add flour and mix in thoroughly.
4. Add double cream (and vanilla extract if using) and mix until completely combined.
5. Line a springform pan with baking parchment — fold it up over the edges since the batter rises.
6. Pour batter into the prepared pan.
7. Bake in a preheated oven at 200°C fan for 45–50 minutes. Check through the oven door — do not open it. Adjust temperature down if your oven runs hot; slight overbrowning on top is acceptable.
8. Remove from oven and cool to room temperature, then refrigerate overnight minimum.
9. Once fully chilled, cut into bite-sized cubes. Keep refrigerated until needed.

Ice Cream Base:
1. Add milk to a pan. Add dextrose, sugar, and stabiliser (if using — pre-mix stabiliser into the sugars before adding). Heat and dissolve completely.
2. Add mascarpone — bring to room temperature first if possible to help it incorporate. Whisk into the warm milk until fully combined.
3. Once fully combined, add skimmed milk powder and mix through.
4. Turn off the heat and add lemon extract. Give a final mix.
5. Finally, stir in the cream (not heated — added off the heat).
6. Transfer to a container and refrigerate until fully chilled, approximately 3–4 hours.
7. Once chilled, give the mix a final stir and churn in an ice cream machine until done.

Assembly:
1. While the ice cream is churning, take the cheesecake cubes out of the fridge.
2. When churning is complete, load the tub by alternating scoops of ice cream with cubes of cheesecake.
3. Top with 2–3 cubes of burnt basque cheesecake to finish.
4. Freeze for 4–5 hours until hardened.

**Notes:**
- Nick over-baked his cheesecake but considered it still excellent — don't be discouraged by imperfect browning.
- Mascarpone is ~40–45% fat, so only a small amount of extra cream is needed; the mascarpone carries the richness.
- Bring mascarpone to room temperature before mixing — cold mascarpone is harder to incorporate into warm milk but will combine eventually with sustained whisking.
- Stabiliser is optional but recommended if you won't eat the whole pint in one sitting.
- Nick's afterthought: make a biscuit base (digestive/graham cracker + butter), freeze it, then break it up and scatter over the top at serving for extra texture.
- The original Crumbs and Doilies recipe called for 200°C fan for 50–60 min; Nick recommends checking from 45 min and adjusting for your oven.
---

### [Butterscotch Honeycomb Ice Cream DIY: Step-by-Step Guide](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=cCIrNFq4QHQ)
- **350g** cream (33% fat)
- **80g** brown sugar
- **10g** dextrose
- **90g** butterscotch sauce (from above)
- **55g** skim milk powder (SMP)
- **1.5g** stabilizer (or 2 egg yolks if no stabilizer)

*Honeycomb Candy (optional mix-in):*
- **200g** white sugar
- **100g** golden syrup (e.g. Rogers, or corn syrup)
- **10g** bicarbonate of soda (baking soda — NOT baking powder)

*Butterscotch Chocolate Crackle (topping):*
- **~50g** 70% dark chocolate + butterscotch chocolate chips (~20–25g chips)
- **~10g** refined coconut oil

---

**Steps:**

1. **Make the butterscotch sauce:** Combine brown sugar, butter, and cream in a saucepan. Bring to a boil, reduce heat, and simmer for 3–4 minutes until flavors meld. Remove from heat and set aside. Do not worry about reaching a specific candy temperature — you are just building flavor, not making candy. Reserve 90g for the base; keep the rest for swirling and topping.

2. **Prepare the dry mix:** Combine the brown sugar, dextrose, and stabilizer (or egg yolks) in a bowl and stir together thoroughly. This prevents the stabilizer from clumping when it hits the liquid.

3. **Make the ice cream base:** Gently warm the milk and cream in a saucepan. Add the milk powder and stir to dissolve. Add the dry sugar/stabilizer mix and stir until fully dissolved. Add 90g of the butterscotch sauce and stir to incorporate. Heat to 80–85°C to ensure the stabilizer hydrates fully. Remove from heat and chill in the fridge.

4. **Make the honeycomb candy:** Add white sugar and golden syrup to a clean dry pan. Warm to 140–150°C (hard crack stage). Remove from heat, add bicarbonate of soda, and stir immediately — the mixture will foam up rapidly. Pour into a lined tray and leave to set completely. Once cooled, break into pieces; crush/powder some for dusting and reserve larger pieces for mix-in and garnish. Store unused honeycomb in an airtight container.

5. **Churn the ice cream:** Churn the chilled base in an ice cream machine. In the last few minutes of churning, briefly microwave the reserved butterscotch sauce (10-second bursts) to loosen it to ~25–30°C pouring consistency.

6. **Pack the container:** Layer the churned ice cream into a tub, drizzling butterscotch sauce between layers. Freeze until completely solid (high sugar content means this takes longer — Nick needed ~4 hours at a slightly colder freezer setting).

7. **Make the crackle topping:** Melt the chocolate and butterscotch chips with the refined coconut oil in the microwave in 10–15 second bursts until smooth (about 1 minute total). Use refined (not virgin) coconut oil to avoid coconut flavor.

8. **Serve:** Scoop into a bowl. Pour the warm butterscotch chocolate crackle over the top — it sets on contact with the cold ice cream. Dust with powdered honeycomb and top with a piece of honeycomb candy.

---

**Notes:**
- Nick notes the butterscotch sauce solidifies overnight at room temperature — it will need a brief microwave to loosen before use.
- The description includes an editorial note: on day 3 in the freezer the high butterfat becomes very noticeable. Nick planned to revise the recipe but states it is beautiful for the first 2 days.
- Critical: use bicarbonate of soda (baking soda), NOT baking powder for the honeycomb. Baking powder produces a flat, dense result with very little aeration.
- The stabilizer can be replaced with 2 egg yolks — egg yolks provide emulsification which is especially important given the high butterfat from the butterscotch sauce.
- Mix the stabilizer with the dry sugars before adding to liquid to prevent clumping.
- This is a traditional churned ice cream recipe, not a Ninja Creami recipe.
---

### [Candy Cane Ice Cream: A Festive Frozen Delight](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8MLXd-NAU8I)
Peppermint ice cream base with candy cane pieces churned in — a classic Canadian/North American festive flavour.

**Ingredients:**
- **500g** milk (2% fat)
- **300g** cream (33% fat)
- **90g** sugar
- **50g** dextrose
- **46g** skim milk powder
- **2g** stabilizer (Special Ingredients brand)
- **10g** peppermint extract
- **2–3** candy canes (broken up, dissolved into the hot base)
- **6** candy canes, crushed (frozen, added as mix-in near end of churn)

**Steps:**
1. Crush 6 candy canes in a pestle and mortar to a mix of medium-to-small pieces and powder — not super fine. Put in a container and freeze.
2. Combine milk and cream in a saucepan over low heat. Add sugar, dextrose, skim milk powder, and stabilizer, stirring to dissolve.
3. Break 2–3 candy canes into the warming liquid to dissolve and infuse flavor.
4. Heat to 75–80°C (required to activate the stabilizer). Turn off heat and allow to tick for a few more minutes.
5. Pour base into a container. Cover and refrigerate until fully chilled.
6. Once cold, add peppermint extract if desired (add when cold, not hot — volatile flavour). Note: the dissolved candy canes may provide enough mint flavour on their own; taste before adding extract.
7. Churn in ice cream machine. About 2–3 minutes before end of churn, add the frozen crushed candy cane pieces.
8. Transfer to storage container and freeze.

**Notes:**
- The 2 candy canes dissolved into the base provided sufficient mint flavour without any peppermint extract — taste your base before adding extract.
- Adding more candy canes to the base is possible but they are pure sugar and will affect the freezing point.
- Candy canes sourced from Canadian Tire.
- Nick describes this as a bigger batch he wants to make again — implying it may be a larger-than-single-pint yield (500g milk + 300g cream = ~800g liquid before other solids).
- Recipe was previously used as the base in Nick's "Minter Wonderland" comparison video (2022).

### [Chasing the Perfect Turkish Ice Cream (Part 2)](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ZSZ02uH91U8)

Attempt at Turkish dondurma (stretchy ice cream) without salep or mastic, using locust bean gum and guar gum — a chef's internet recipe that Nick tested unmodified and found deeply disappointing.

**Ingredients:**
- **335g** whole milk
- **335g** heavy cream
- **100g** sugar
- **3g** locust bean gum (lo auasal / gallan gum) — G&G brand used
- **3g** guar gum — G&G brand, flavor-free version recommended
- **1 pinch** salt
- **5g** vanilla extract

**Steps:**
1. Pre-chill your ice cream machine bowl.
2. Combine milk and cream in a mixing bowl.
3. In a separate small bowl, whisk together sugar, locust bean gum, guar gum, and salt until evenly mixed.
4. Add the dry mix to the liquid and whisk vigorously for 2 minutes.
5. Add vanilla extract (Nick forgot initially and added it after pouring into the machine — either step works).
6. Churn in a traditional ice cream machine until it reaches soft-serve consistency similar to the original salep-based version.
7. Transfer to a pre-chilled bowl and whip for a further 2 minutes to develop any stretch texture.
8. Decant into a freezer container and freeze for at least 2 days.

**Notes:**
- This is a **failed recipe** — Nick tested it as-is without adjustments and explicitly says not to waste time or money making it. It is icy, dry, and crumbles apart in shards rather than scooping cohesively.
- Root cause: locust bean gum requires heat activation to function properly; this recipe uses no heat, so the gum is wasted. The guar gum level is also insufficient to produce meaningful stretch on its own.
- Part 1 (with real salep and mastic): https://youtu.be/O7xOlzCmi28?si=KhFE6BzfcoHNYvKu
- Part 3 (Nick's own corrected dondurma recipe) is planned for a later video.
- This is a **traditional ice cream machine** recipe, not a Ninja Creami recipe.
- The original recipe was sourced from a chef's online class; Nick intentionally withholds the source to avoid calling them out publicly.
---

### [Chicken & Waffle Ice Cream](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=boeFYmuu8FU)

Maple syrup ice cream with chicken-fat-fried Liege waffle pieces — a savory-sweet combo inspired by Salt & Straw and Made By Marcus, but done Nick's way.

**Ingredients:**

Ice cream base:
- **400g** milk (1.8–2% fat)
- **500g** double/heavy cream
- **30g** light brown sugar
- **150g** maple syrup (real maple syrup — not maple-type syrup)
- **67g** SMP (skimmed milk powder)
- **100g** dextrose
- **1.5g** stabilizer (guar gum + locust bean gum, pre-mixed into the sugar)

Waffle pieces:
- Liege waffles (quantity to taste), chopped into cubes
- Chicken fat / schmaltz (rendered — enough to coat and fry the waffle pieces)
- Small amount of sugar (for final caramelization toss)

**Steps:**

1. Add milk to a saucepan. Pre-mix the stabilizer (guar gum + locust bean gum) into the light brown sugar to prevent clumping, then add the sugar mix to the pan.
2. Add dextrose and maple syrup. Slowly bring up to temperature, stirring to dissolve the sugars — no eggs in this recipe, so no specific target temperature, just dissolve everything well.
3. Add the skimmed milk powder (SMP) and stir until fully dissolved into the base.
4. Remove from heat and transfer to a container. Add double cream, give a final stir, and refrigerate for a few hours.
5. Chop Liege waffles into small cube-sized pieces.
6. Melt a generous amount of chicken schmaltz (rendered chicken fat) in a frying pan. Add waffle pieces and fry for ~5 minutes, allowing the fat to soak into the starch. Add more fat as needed.
7. Drain off excess fat, then continue cooking for another minute or so to crisp up the cubes.
8. Sprinkle a small amount of sugar over the waffle pieces and toss in the pan for ~30 seconds to caramelize. Remove from heat and cool completely.
9. Churn the ice cream base in your ice cream machine. In the last few minutes of churning, add the cooled chicken-fried waffle pieces and mix through until combined.
10. Tub up and freeze.

**Notes:**

- Dextrose is used instead of sucrose for a softer, more scoopable texture — less hardness from the freezer.
- Pre-mixing gums into sugar before adding to liquid prevents clumping.
- Double cream is added off the heat (after the base is removed from the stove), which is Nick's standard method.
- Chicken schmaltz is just rendered chicken fat — a jarred product. The fat is deliberately soaked into the waffle starch, not just used as a fry medium.
- The chicken element is subtle and in the background — by design. It's not an aggressive chicken flavor; it's savory depth from the fat-fried waffle.
- The sugar toss at the end gives a sweet-bite caramelized crunch to the waffle pieces that contrasts the maple ice cream.
- Use real maple syrup — maple-type syrup tastes poor and may require switching flavors altogether.
- Nick notes the waffle pieces retain good crunch even after churning and freezing.
- Serve with Liege waffles on the side and maple syrup drizzle as topping to complete the classic chicken-and-waffle trio.
---
